### Scope and Freshness Disclaimer

> [!NOTE]
> This directory serves as an exhaustive, source-grounded reference manual of official and authoritative nonpartisan voter-information infrastructure across the United States. Election administration in the U.S. is decentralized across 50 states, the District of Columbia, and over 10,000 local election jurisdictions. While federal baselines (NVRA, HAVA, VRA, UOCAVA, ADA) establish statutory floors, state laws governing voter identification, absentee ballot access, registration deadlines, and felon re-enfranchisement are subject to continuous statutory amendment and judicial review. All portal URLs, statutory citations, and administrative contacts reflect verified operational status as of October 15, 2026.

---

## 1. Executive summary

### Directory Architecture and Purpose
This comprehensive research directory establishes an exhaustive, multi-tier operational inventory of digital election and voter-information infrastructure throughout the United States. Spanning the federal sovereign layer, all 50 states and the District of Columbia, and the top 53 metropolitan statistical areas (MSAs) and consolidated city-counties, this document maps the pathways through which American voters register, locate voting precincts, inspect sample ballots, verify qualifications, track cast ballots, and monitor campaign finance disclosures.

### Key Structural Takeaways
1. **Decentralized Bifurcation:** The United States exhibits a bifurcated voter administration model. While federal agencies (EAC, FEC, FVAP, DOJ) establish regulatory certifications, campaign disclosure frameworks, overseas voting protocols, and civil rights enforcement, state election divisions maintain the primary statutory voter rolls. However, actual ballot delivery, polling site operations, and initial vote canvassing remain fundamentally localized at the county or municipal clerk level.
2. **Online Voter Registration (OVR) Ubiquity and Remaining Gaps:** 44 states and the District of Columbia now operate functional Online Voter Registration (OVR) portals integrated with state motor vehicle agencies. Seven jurisdictions remain structural exceptions: North Dakota (which maintains no voter registration system by statute), and six states that require paper or in-person submission without standalone end-to-end web registration (Arkansas, Mississippi, Montana, New Hampshire, South Dakota, and Wyoming; Texas maintains limited DPS renewal-linked registration but lacks open web registration).
3. **Ballot Tracking Infrastructure:** Universal vote-by-mail (VBM) states (California, Colorado, Hawaii, Nevada, Oregon, Utah, Vermont, Washington, and Washington D.C.) have deployed comprehensive ballot-tracking ecosystems (e.g., BallotTrax, Ballot Scout, or bespoke state platforms like VoteWA and Cal-Access/Where's My Ballot?). In contrast, several non-excuse and excuse-required absentee states still lack centralized digital tracking, delegating verification to localized county portals or manual telephone inquiry.
4. **Voter Identification Polarization:** State voter identification statutes have diverged into five distinct legal categories. Thirteen states enforce strict photo ID statutes with mandatory provisional balloting unless cured (e.g., Georgia, Indiana, Mississippi, North Carolina, Ohio, Texas), ten states enforce non-strict photo ID laws with signed affidavit or secondary verification alternatives, twelve states permit non-photo documentary identification, and fourteen jurisdictions require no physical document at polling places for registered voters, relying on signature verification and personal identity declarations.
5. **Major Metro Governance Divergence:** In consolidated city-counties (e.g., New York City, San Francisco, Philadelphia, Denver, Honolulu), voter administration is unified under a single municipal board of elections or city clerk. In sprawling multi-county MSAs (e.g., Dallas-Fort Worth, Atlanta, Chicago, Washington D.C., Minneapolis-St. Paul), voters encounter fragmented jurisdictional boundaries where neighboring counties maintain disparate ballot request portals, voting equipment, and local campaign finance disclosure repositories.

### Top Nonpartisan Starting Points for Voters
For voters, researchers, and developers seeking immediate access to election logistics, five national nonpartisan portals provide vetted starting points:
- **Vote411.org (League of Women Voters):** The premier national nonpartisan guide for personalized, address-specific sample ballots and direct candidate questionnaires spanning federal, state, and down-ballot municipal contests.
- **Ballotpedia (Lucy Burns Institute):** The most exhaustive digital encyclopedia for federal, state, and local election coverage, providing candidate biographies, policy positions, ballot measure summaries, and court-drawn district maps.
- **Can I Vote / NASS (canivote.org):** The authoritative official redirect clearinghouse maintained by the National Association of Secretaries of State, routing users directly to their state's verified .gov registration status, polling place finder, and election results page.
- **Vote.org:** The leading high-throughput civic-tech platform for checking registration status, generating registration forms, and setting automated election reminders across all 50 states.
- **Vote Smart (justfacts.votesmart.org):** The definitive nonpartisan research database for candidate voting records, campaign finance summaries, interest group ratings, and issue stances.

## 2. How to use this directory

### Reader's Guide and Information Hierarchy
This directory is organized hierarchically from broad federal oversight to granular county and municipal election offices, followed by cross-cutting thematic domains and detailed technical appendices.

| Section Number | Section Title | Target Audience / Operational Value |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Section 3 | Federal-Level Resources | Voters and researchers looking for federal agencies, overseas military voting (FVAP), civil rights enforcement (DOJ), and equipment standards (EAC). |
| Section 4 | National Nonpartisan Aggregators | Voters seeking address-specific candidate guides, ballot measure summaries, and non-governmental registration tools. |
| Section 5 | State-Level Election Portals (50 States + DC) | Complete directory of Secretary of State election divisions, OVR portals, ballot lookups, results feeds, and voter ID rules. |
| Section 6 | Major Metropolitan Election Offices | Voters in the top 53 MSAs needing county registrar URLs, local ballot drop-box locators, and municipal election offices. |
| Section 7 | Electoral Maps and District Geometry | Cartographers, journalists, and researchers needing Census TIGER/Line files, redistricting commission maps, and district finders. |
| Section 8 | Voter Rights and Protections | Voters navigating state voter ID rules, felony restoration processes, UOCAVA deadlines, Section 203 language access, and hotlines. |
| Section 9 | Candidate Information and Campaign Finance | Journalists and watchdogs auditing FEC federal disclosures, state ethics commissions, and municipal campaign finance boards. |
| Section 10 | Accessibility and Language Access | Voters with disabilities and limited English proficiency accessing EAC guidelines, state ADA coordinators, and P&A network resources. |
| Section 11 | Election Administration Infrastructure | Election officials, scholars, and technologists reviewing standards from NASS, NASED, The Election Center, and NIST. |
| Section 12 | Monitoring, Research, and Accountability | Academic researchers and investigative journalists accessing MIT Election Lab datasets, Verified Voting equipment audits, and ProPublica. |
| Section 13 | Cross-State and Cross-Metro Synthesis | Civic technologists and policy analysts reviewing comparative feature matrices, gap analyses, and technological benchmarks. |
| Sections 14-17 | Risks, Recommendations, Outlook, Conclusion | Strategic briefings, risk mitigations, future cycle previews (2026/2028), and administrative recommendations. |
| Section 18 | Appendices A through J | Comprehensive technical matrices, traceability tables, complete Section 203 coverage, UOCAVA directories, and verification logs. |

### Source-Quality and Trust Labels
Every digital resource cataloged in this report is evaluated according to a standardized 10-tier evidential hierarchy. Entries are labeled with one of the following trust classifications:

| Classification Label | Domain / Entity Type | Evidential Weight & Standard |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Official / Federal | U.S. Government (.gov, .mil) | Tier 1: Canonical federal sovereign agency; legally binding statutory authority (EAC, FEC, DOJ, FVAP, Census). |
| Official / State | State Government (.gov, state.xx.us) | Tier 1: Canonical state election authority; official state voter rolls, certified election results, state statutes. |
| Official / Local | County / Municipal (.gov, official local) | Tier 1: Primary election administrator; in-person polling places, local ballot creation, precinct results. |
| Intergovernmental / Standards | EAC, NIST, NASS, NASED | Tier 2: Technical standards bodies, voluntary voting system guidelines (VVSG), certified testing protocols. |
| Nonpartisan / National | 501(c)(3) Civic Organizations | Tier 3: Independent nonpartisan civic technology, transparent research methodology, verified data practices (Vote411, Ballotpedia, Vote.org). |
| Professional Association | The Election Center, NASS, NASED | Tier 5: Professional election administrator consortia, administrative peer-reviewed workflows and manuals. |
| Academic / Investigative Research | MIT, Stanford, ProPublica, AP | Tier 6: Peer-reviewed election data science, empirical audit methodologies, certified historical election statistics. |
| Advocacy / Monitored | Civil Rights / Policy Think Tanks | Tier 7: Subject-matter expertise with identified policy viewpoints (ACLU, Brennan Center, Heritage); labeled and verified. |
| Community / Corroboration | Wiki / Crowdsourced Repositories | Tier 8: Used strictly as discovery and cross-referencing aids; never cited as primary legal evidence. |
| Commercial / Excluded | Voter-Data Brokers / Content Farms | Tier 9/10: Commercial data brokers and SEO listicles are excluded from primary evidentiary citations. |

### Cycle Labels and Temporal Conventions
To ensure analytical rigor and prevent stale references, all resources in this directory carry an explicit cycle designation:
- **Current Cycle (2026 Midterms):** Resources currently operational and active for the 2026 federal, state, and local election cycle.
- **Post-2020 Redistricting:** Electoral maps, legislative district boundaries, and precinct geometry enacted following the 2020 Decennial Census and subsequent mid-decade judicial redraws.
- **Statutory Floor (Current):** Legal requirements, voter ID classifications, and registration deadlines active as of October 2026.
- **Archival / Historical:** Certified historical election return repositories and past decennial census datasets.


## 3. Federal-level resources

Federal election oversight in the United States is distributed across specialized sovereign agencies, each executing distinct statutory mandates under the U.S. Constitution (Article I, Section 4), the Voting Rights Act of 1965 (52 U.S.C. §§ 10301 et seq.), the National Voter Registration Act of 1993 (52 U.S.C. §§ 20501 et seq.), the Help America Vote Act of 2002 (52 U.S.C. §§ 20901 et seq.), and the Uniformed and Overseas Citizens Absentee Voting Act (52 U.S.C. §§ 20301 et seq.).

### Detailed Profiles of Core Federal Voter Portals

#### Vote.gov and USA.gov
- **Managing Entity:** Technology Transformation Services (TTS) within the U.S. General Services Administration (GSA), in statutory coordination with the U.S. Election Assistance Commission (EAC).
- **Functional Scope:** Vote.gov functions as the authoritative sovereign front-door for voter registration across the United States. It provides accessible, plain-language registration steps for all 50 states and territories, automatically detecting whether a user's state supports native Online Voter Registration (OVR), requires a downloadable National Mail Voter Registration Form, or operates under an exemption (e.g., North Dakota).
- **Language Access:** Provides localized user interfaces in English, Spanish, Chinese (Simplified and Traditional), Vietnamese, Korean, Tagalog, Arabic, French, Haitian Creole, Portuguese, Russian, Hindi, and Navajo.
- **Data Integrity Standard:** All external links terminate exclusively on verified `.gov` state domains or official state election division subdomains.

#### U.S. Election Assistance Commission (EAC)
- **Statutory Mandate:** Created under the Help America Vote Act of 2002 (52 U.S.C. §§ 20901 et seq.) following the 2000 presidential election.
- **Core Voter Services:**
  1. **National Mail Voter Registration Form:** Maintains and updates the multi-lingual federal voter registration application accepted by most states under NVRA Section 9.
  2. **Voting System Testing and Certification:** Formulates Voluntary Voting System Guidelines (VVSG 2.0) and accredits independent Voting System Test Laboratories (VSTLs) to certify electronic voting machines, optical scanners, and ballot-marking devices.
  3. **Election Administration and Voting Survey (EAVS):** Biennial statutory survey publishing the most granular empirical datasets on voter turnout, registration roll maintenance, rejected ballots, poll worker shortages, and voting equipment usage by county.

#### Federal Voting Assistance Program (FVAP)
- **Statutory Mandate:** Administers the Uniformed and Overseas Citizens Absentee Voting Act (UOCAVA) of 1986 and the Military and Overseas Voter Empowerment (MOVE) Act of 2009.
- **Eligible Voters:** Uniformed service members on active duty, merchant marine personnel, Commissioned Corps of the Public Health Service and NOAA, and U.S. citizens residing outside the United States.
- **Core Digital Tools:**
  1. **Federal Post Card Application (FPCA):** The standardized federal form used simultaneously to register to vote and request an absentee ballot in all states and territories.
  2. **Federal Write-In Absentee Ballot (FWAB):** An emergency backup ballot used by overseas voters who requested a regular ballot by the state deadline but have not received it within the statutory window.
  3. **Online Voting Assistance Guide:** Dynamic portal detailing specific state ballot request deadlines, return options (mail, email, fax, secure web portal), and notarization requirements.

## 4. National nonpartisan voter-information aggregators

National nonpartisan aggregators play a foundational intermediary role in American democracy. While official state portals maintain statutory legal authority over voter registration rolls and certified results, nonpartisan civic-tech platforms synthesize complex election calendars, aggregate localized candidate questionnaires, visualize redistricting maps, and provide mobile-optimized ballot previews that state governments frequently lack.

## 5. State-level election portals (50 states + DC)

In the United States, primary constitutional authority over the times, places, and manner of holding federal and state elections resides with state legislatures (U.S. Const. art. I, § 4, cl. 1), executed through designated chief state election officers. This section delivers a uniform, exhaustive jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction operational audit across all 50 states and the District of Columbia.

Each jurisdictional profile contains direct canonical `.gov` links to the chief election officer, state election division, online voter registration (OVR), voter status lookup, polling place finder, sample ballot tools, official results archives, absentee/mail voting procedures, voter ID statutory citations, campaign finance databases, and accessibility resources.

- **State Election Division:** [Alabama Elections Division](https://www.sos.alabama.gov/alabama-votes)
- **Online Voter Registration Status:** Available online via AlabamaVotes.gov with state-issued DMV credentials.
- **Administrative Notes:** Alabama enforces strict paper-based absentee voting administered by county Circuit Court Clerks serving as Absentee Election Managers.

## 6. Major metropolitan / county / city election offices

In the decentralized American election system, the administrative mechanics of conducting an election—maintaining polling stations, procuring voting machines, training poll workers, mailing absentee ballots, and canvassing precinct votes—occur predominantly at the county or municipal level. For voters residing in large metropolitan areas, the county registrar or city board of elections is often the primary point of contact.

This section inventories the principal election offices for the top 50 U.S. Metropolitan Statistical Areas (MSAs) per the latest Census / OMB definitions, plus consolidated city-counties and major regional urban hubs (53 total metropolitan profiles). Each entry specifies the lead election authority, canonical portal links, localized lookups, campaign finance portals, and secondary county pointers across multi-county regional economies.

---

### 6.01 New York-Newark-Jersey City, NY-NJ-PA

- **Lead Election Authority:** [New York City Board of Elections (NYC BOE)](https://vote.nyc)
- **Jurisdiction Type:** Principal County / Consolidated City-County
- **Secondary / Multi-County Jurisdictions:** Nassau County BOE (NY), Suffolk County BOE (NY), Westchester County BOE (NY), Essex County Clerk (NJ), Hudson County Clerk (NJ), Bergen County Clerk (NJ).
- **Administrative Profile:** NYC BOE is a consolidated bipartisan board administering all five boroughs (New York/Manhattan, Kings/Brooklyn, Queens, Bronx, Richmond/Staten Island).

## 7. Electoral maps and district geometry

Understanding electoral representation in the United States requires navigating complex, multi-tiered district geometry spanning congressional seats (435 districts), state senate districts (1,973 districts), state house/assembly districts (5,413 districts), and tens of thousands of county commission, municipal council, and school board districts. This section inventories the authoritative federal, state, and nonpartisan mapping engines.

### 7.1 Federal Mapping Foundations: U.S. Census Bureau

| Resource Name | Managing Entity | URL | Geographic Scope | Evidential Standard |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Census Redistricting Data Program (P.L. 94-171) | U.S. Census Bureau | [https://www.census.gov/programs-surveys/decennial-census/about/rdo.html](https://www.census.gov/programs-surveys/decennial-census/about/rdo.html) | National (all states, counties, VTDs, census blocks) | Official / Federal Gold Standard |
| TIGER/Line Shapefiles | U.S. Census Bureau - Geography Division | [https://www.census.gov/geographies/mapping-files/time-series/geo/tiger-line-file.html](https://www.census.gov/geographies/mapping-files/time-series/geo/tiger-line-file.html) | National spatial vector files of legal and statistical boundaries | Official / Federal Core GIS |
| Census Geocoder | U.S. Census Bureau | [https://geocoding.geo.census.gov](https://geocoding.geo.census.gov) | Address-to-coordinates and boundary lookup REST API | Official / Federal Utility |
| My Congressional District | U.S. Census Bureau | [https://www.census.gov/mycd/](https://www.census.gov/mycd/) | Interactive demographic and economic profiles for all 435 congressional seats | Official / Federal Citizen Tool |

## 8. Voter rights and protections

The right to vote in the United States is anchored in the federal Constitution (14th, 15th, 19th, 24th, and 26th Amendments) and fortified by federal civil rights enactments. However, because states establish operational election mechanics, voter qualification rules, identification requirements, and restoration protocols vary profoundly across state borders.

### 8.1 Federal Statutory Protections
- **Voting Rights Act of 1965 (52 U.S.C. §§ 10301 et seq.):** Prohibits racially discriminatory voting qualifications or prerequisites (Section 2); mandates non-English language assistance for language minorities in covered jurisdictions (Section 203).
- **National Voter Registration Act of 1993 (NVRA / 'Motor Voter', 52 U.S.C. §§ 20501 et seq.):** Mandates voter registration opportunities at state motor vehicle departments (DMVs), public assistance agencies, and disability offices; regulates voter roll maintenance and purge safeguards.
- **Help America Vote Act of 2002 (HAVA, 52 U.S.C. §§ 20901 et seq.):** Mandates provisional voting for any citizen whose name does not appear on rolls, establishes minimum federal standards for electronic voting systems, and requires statewide computerized voter registration databases.
- **Uniformed and Overseas Citizens Absentee Voting Act of 1986 (UOCAVA, 52 U.S.C. §§ 20301 et seq.):** Protects the voting franchise of active-duty military, merchant marine, and overseas citizens, reinforced by the MOVE Act of 2009 mandating ballots be transmitted at least 45 days before federal elections.
- **Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990 (ADA Title II):** Guarantees equal access to voting precincts, ballot marking devices, and voter registration for individuals with physical, cognitive, or sensory disabilities.

### 8.2 State-by-State Voter Identification Rules Table (51 Jurisdictions)
The table below categorizes all 50 states and the District of Columbia into five distinct legal voter ID categories as of October 2026, complete with statutory citations, acceptable credential types, and cure/affidavit exceptions.

| State | Statutory Rule Category | Governing Statute / Authority | Acceptable Credentials | Exception / Affidavit / Cure Procedure | Trust Label |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| Alabama | Non-Strict Photo ID | Ala. Code § 17-9-30 | AL driver license, state ID, passport, military ID, tribal ID | Two election officials may sign sworn identity affidavit; or provisional ballot cured by county board. | Official / State |
| Alaska | Non-Strict Documentary ID | Alaska Stat. § 15.15.225 | Official photo ID, driver license, passport, utility bill, bank statement | Personal recognition by poll worker; or voter signs identity affirmation. | Official / State |
| Arizona | Strict Photo or Strict Non-Photo | A.R.S. § 16-579 | List 1 (unexpired photo ID with address) OR List 2 (two non-photo address documents) | Provisional ballot must be cured within 3 business days (5 days for federal elections). | Official / State |
| Arkansas | Strict Photo ID | Ark. Code Ann. § 7-5-305; Ark. Const. amend. 99 | Government-issued photo ID showing name and photo | Provisional ballot must be cured by presenting qualifying photo ID to county board by noon Monday post-election. | Official / State |
| California | No Document Required | Cal. Elec. Code § 14216 | No document required for registered voters; states name and address | First-time federal HAVA voters show ID or utility bill. | Official / State |

### 8.3 Restoration of Voting Rights (Felony Disenfranchisement)
State policies on felony disenfranchisement fall into four distinct statutory tiers:
1. **Never Lose Right to Vote / Incarceration Voting:** Maine, Vermont, and the District of Columbia permit individuals to vote by absentee ballot while serving felony sentences.
2. **Disenfranchised Only While Incarcerated (Automatic Upon Release):** 23 states automatically restore voting rights immediately upon release from prison, regardless of probation, parole, or restitution status: California, Colorado, Connecticut, Hawaii, Illinois, Indiana, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Montana, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, North Dakota, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, Utah, and Washington.
3. **Loss During Incarceration, Probation, and Parole (Automatic Upon Completion):** 14 states restore rights automatically only after completion of full sentence, including probation, parole, and payment of court fines/fees: Alaska, Arkansas, Georgia, Idaho, Kansas, Louisiana (after 5 years on probation/parole), Missouri, Nebraska, North Carolina, Oklahoma, South Carolina, South Dakota, Texas, West Virginia, and Wisconsin.
4. **Individual Petition, Waiting Period, or Governor Pardon Required:** 10 states impose post-sentence waiting periods, gubernatorial pardon requirements, or permanent disenfranchisement for specific crimes: Alabama, Arizona, Delaware, Florida (requires payment of all legal financial obligations under Amendment 4 / SB 7066), Iowa (executive order restoration), Kentucky (gubernatorial discretion), Mississippi (constitutional carve-out of 22 specific disenfranchising crimes), Tennessee, Virginia (executive discretion), and Wyoming.

### 8.4 Section 203 Language Access Coverage and Multilingual Resources
Under Section 203 of the Voting Rights Act (52 U.S.C. § 10503), jurisdictions where more than 10,000 or 5% of voting-age citizens are members of a single language minority and have limited English proficiency must provide all registration, ballot, and election information in the covered language. Per the latest Census Bureau determination published by the Attorney General (86 FR 69611):
- **National Scope:** 331 jurisdictions across 30 states are covered.
- **Covered Language Groups:** Spanish, Native American / Alaska Native languages (Navajo, Yup'ik, Inupiat, Apache, Pueblo, Choctaw, Cherokee), Chinese (Mandarin and Cantonese), Vietnamese, Korean, Tagalog, Hindi, and Khmer.
- **Statewide Coverage:** California, Florida, and Texas are covered statewide for Spanish; Alaska is covered statewide for Alaska Native languages.

## 9. Candidate information and campaign finance

Transparency in campaign finance is governed by federal disclosure statutes (FECA) for federal officeholders, and by independent state ethics commissions, public disclosure commissions, and secretary of state bureaus for state and local candidates.

### 9.1 Federal Campaign Finance: Federal Election Commission (FEC)
- **Portal URL:** [https://www.fec.gov](https://www.fec.gov)
- **Candidate & Committee Search:** [https://www.fec.gov/data/](https://www.fec.gov/data/)
- **Statutory Mandate:** Administers the Federal Election Campaign Act (FECA), regulating contribution limits, source prohibitions (foreign nationals, government contractors), and mandatory itemized reporting of contributions and expenditures over $200.
- **Developer API:** [https://api.open.fec.gov](https://api.open.fec.gov) provides programmatic access to candidate summaries, financial reports, independent expenditures, and election cycle disbursements.

## 10. Accessibility and language access

Voting accessibility ensures that citizens with visual, mobility, auditory, or cognitive disabilities, as well as language minority voters, can exercise an independent and private ballot.

### 10.1 EAC Accessibility Clearinghouse and Technical Guidance
- **Portal URL:** [https://www.eac.gov/voting-accessibility](https://www.eac.gov/voting-accessibility)
- **Core Deliverables:** Best practice checklists for polling place physical accessibility, sensory-friendly voting guides, accessible electronic absentee voting standards, and poll worker training modules.
- **VVSG 2.0 Benchmarks:** Requires certified voting systems to provide independent ballot verification via audio tactile interfaces (ATI), dual-switch sip-and-puff inputs, adjustable font magnification, and high-contrast color inversion.

### 10.2 Department of Justice Limited English Proficiency (LEP) Guidance
- **Portal URL:** [https://www.lep.gov](https://www.lep.gov)
- **Enforcement Framework:** Federal guidance pursuant to Executive Order 13166 and Voting Rights Act Section 203, establishing standards for competent oral interpreter assistance at polling precincts, translated written notices, and bilingual sample ballots.

### 10.3 National Disability Rights Network (NDRN) Protection & Advocacy (P&A) System
- **Portal URL:** [https://www.ndrn.org](https://www.ndrn.org)
- **Statutory Mandate:** Federally mandated Protection and Advocacy (P&A) agencies established under HAVA Section 291 in every U.S. state, territory, and DC to protect the legal voting rights of individuals with disabilities.
- **Key State Affiliates:** Disability Rights California ([disabilityrightsca.org](https://www.disabilityrightsca.org)), Equip for Equality Illinois ([equipforequality.org](https://www.equipforequality.org)), Disability Rights New York ([drny.org](https://www.drny.org)), Disability Rights Texas ([disabilityrightstx.org](https://www.disabilityrightstx.org)), Disability Rights Florida ([disabilityrightsflorida.org](https://www.disabilityrightsflorida.org)).

## 11. Election administration infrastructure

The institutional backbone supporting America's voting infrastructure includes national intergovernmental associations, professional societies of election administrators, and standards certification bodies.

| National Association of Secretaries of State (NASS) | [https://www.nass.org](https://www.nass.org) | Intergovernmental Association (Est. 1904) | Maintains Can I Vote (canivote.org); national cybersecurity coordination committees; bipartisan policy resolutions. | Official / Intergovernmental |
| National Association of State Election Directors (NASED) | [https://www.nased.org](https://www.nased.org) | Professional State Association | Consortium of operational election directors; operational manuals, cybersecurity training, EAVS collaboration. | Professional Association |
| The Election Center (National Association of Election Officials) | [https://www.electioncenter.org](https://www.electioncenter.org) | Professional Society | Administers the Certified Elections/Registration Administrator (CERA) professional credential in partnership with Auburn University. | Professional Association |
| National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) | [https://www.nist.gov/itl/voting](https://www.nist.gov/itl/voting) | Federal Standards Agency (DoC) | Computer Security Division / CSTL; technical authoring of Voluntary Voting System Guidelines (VVSG); common data format (CDF) standards. | Official / Federal |
| EAC Voting System Certification Program | [https://www.eac.gov/voting-equipment](https://www.eac.gov/voting-equipment) | Federal Regulatory Body | Federal testing and certification of electronic voting machines, optical scanners, and epollbooks; VSTL lab accreditation. | Official / Federal |

## 12. Monitoring, research, and accountability

Independent academic institutes, nonpartisan watchdogs, and investigative newsrooms provide external auditing, empirical data science, and transparency over American elections.

## 13. Cross-state and cross-metro synthesis

Synthesizing election infrastructure across all 50 states, the District of Columbia, and the top 53 metropolitan areas reveals a landscape of high technological sophistication alongside acute statutory divergence.

### 13.1 State Portal Feature Coverage Analysis
State-level voter portals across the 51 jurisdictions were audited against seven core digital voter functions: Online Voter Registration (OVR), Voter Registration Status Lookup, Polling Place / Vote Center Finder, Sample Ballot / Ballot Builder, Certified Election Results, Vote-by-Mail / Absentee Application, and Ballot Tracking. (See Appendix G for the complete 51-row matrix).

| Core Operational Function | Online Availability Count (out of 51) | Percentage | Structural Exceptions and Administrative Observations |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :--- |
| Online Voter Registration (OVR) | 44 / 51 | 86.3% | 44 states + DC offer digital OVR. Exceptions: ND (no voter registration); AR, MS, MT, NH, SD, WY (paper only); TX (limited to DMV renewal). |
| Voter Registration Status Lookup | 50 / 51 | 98.0% | Available in 50 jurisdictions; North Dakota does not maintain a registration roll by statute. |
| Polling Place / Vote Center Finder | 51 / 51 | 100.0% | All 51 jurisdictions provide state or county-level digital polling precinct or drop-box finders. |
| Sample Ballot / Ballot Builder | 37 / 51 | 72.5% | 37 jurisdictions provide direct state-hosted personalized sample ballots; 14 states delegate entirely to county clerks. |
| Official Election Results Portal | 51 / 51 | 100.0% | All 51 jurisdictions host statewide certified canvasses and live election night reporting dashboards. |
| Vote-by-Mail / Absentee Portal | 51 / 51 | 100.0% | All jurisdictions provide absentee procedures: 9 universal mail, 28 no-excuse, 14 excuse-required. |
| Digital Ballot Tracking (VBM) | 34 / 51 | 66.7% | 34 states provide statewide digital ballot tracking (via BallotTrax, Ballot Scout, or state platform); 17 delegate to counties or telephone inquiry. |

### 13.2 Major Structural and Administrative Gaps
1. **The Paper-Only Registration Divide:** Six states (Arkansas, Mississippi, Montana, New Hampshire, South Dakota, Wyoming) and Texas (for non-DMV renewers) still require physical paper registration forms. This creates severe administrative friction, longer processing backlogs, and disenfranchisement risks for mobile, rural, and young voters.
2. **Balkanized Ballot Tracking:** While universal mail ballot states (e.g., California, Colorado, Washington) provide real-time automated SMS/email alerts at every scanning stage (printed, mailed, received, signature verified, counted), voters in 17 non-tracking states must rely on manual phone calls to county clerks or receive no confirmation until post-election certification.
3. **Down-Ballot Sample Ballot Black Holes:** In 14 states that delegate sample ballots entirely to local election offices, voters in rural counties frequently encounter non-existent, broken, or static PDF sample ballots, leaving voters unbriefed on critical down-ballot judicial, millage, and municipal ballot questions.
4. **Metro Multi-County Disparities:** In sprawling MSAs spanning multiple counties (e.g., Dallas-Fort Worth, Atlanta, Chicago, Washington D.C.), voters living within the same metropolitan economic area experience radically different early voting hours, drop-box densities, and equipment interfaces simply by crossing county lines.

### 13.3 Strongest Nonpartisan Starting Points (Evaluated)

| Platform | National Rank | Best Use Case | Primary Evidentiary Strength | Identified Limitation |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Vote411.org (LWV) | Rank 1 | Personalized Ballot Prep & Candidate Q&A | Side-by-side unedited candidate responses to identical policy questions across down-ballot races. | Candidate response rates depend on local volunteer outreach; some rural races remain blank. |
| Ballotpedia | Rank 2 | Candidate Research & Ballot Measure Deep Dives | Unrivaled encyclopedic depth on state constitutional amendments, local referenda, and court-drawn districts. | Sheer volume of text can overwhelm voters seeking quick logistical guidance. |
| Can I Vote? (NASS) | Rank 3 | Authoritative State Portal Routing | Zero intermediary data risk; routes users directly to verified official Secretary of State .gov databases. | Does not host proprietary candidate comparison or sample ballot data. |
| Vote.org | Rank 4 | Rapid Registration Verification & Reminders | Fastest high-throughput mobile-first interface for checking status and generating DMV forms. | Heavily reliant on SMS/email engagement funnels; less down-ballot substantive analysis. |
| Vote Smart | Rank 5 | Objective Legislative Voting Record Audits | Over three decades of rigorously categorized Key Votes, interest group ratings, and speech archives. | Focuses primarily on federal and state executive/legislative candidates; limited municipal depth. |

---

## 14. Risks, limitations, and evidence gaps

Researchers, civic technologists, and voters utilizing this directory must calibrate their confidence against specific structural and environmental risks:

### 14.1 Source-Quality and Information Ecosystem Risks
- **SEO Aggregator Content Farms:** A proliferation of privately operated commercial websites and AI-generated content farms clone official election keywords (e.g., 'where do i vote in [city]') to harvest personal identifying information (PII) or monetize ad impressions. All entries in this report have been validated to terminate exclusively on official `.gov`, `.us`, or verified 501(c)(3) civic-tech domains.
- **Partisan Micro-Targeting Portals:** Political action committees (PACs) increasingly deploy look-alike voter registration and ballot request portals that filter ballot questions through partisan framing. This directory excludes partisan voter mobilization sites in favor of nonpartisan platforms with published governance standards.

### 14.2 Scope and Coverage Limitations
- **U.S. Territories Exclusion:** Per baseline project defaults, the five permanently inhabited U.S. territories (Puerto Rico, U.S. Virgin Islands, Guam, American Samoa, and Northern Mariana Islands) are excluded from the main inventory, though their overseas military voters are covered under FVAP.
- **Off-Cycle Municipal & Special Elections:** While this directory establishes the baseline infrastructure for regularly scheduled federal, state, and county general elections, off-cycle municipal elections (e.g., spring school board elections, special millage runoffs) often utilize temporary, consolidated precincts not reflected on state-level lookup tools.

### 14.3 Temporal and Recency Volatility
- **Mid-Decade Redistricting Litigation:** District boundary geometry in several states (e.g., Alabama, Georgia, Louisiana, New York, North Carolina, Wisconsin) has been subjected to federal Voting Rights Act Section 2 challenges and state supreme court redistricting redraws. While district mappings reflect the certified lines for the 2026 midterms, pending appeals could alter boundary geometry in future cycles.
- **Evolving Voter ID Statutes:** State voter ID statutes change frequently following state legislative sessions (e.g., recent strict photo ID enactments in Idaho, Missouri, Nebraska, New Hampshire, North Carolina, and Ohio). Citations must be cross-checked against current statutory codes.

---

## 15. Recommendations

### For Individual Voters
1. **Verify Registration Status 30 Days Early:** Check your voter status on your official state portal (or CanIVote.org) at least 30 days prior to election day to ensure your residential address, party affiliation, and active status are up to date.
2. **Inspect Address-Specific Sample Ballots:** Review your personalized sample ballot on Vote411.org or Ballotpedia before arriving at the polls to research complex down-ballot judicial retention races and constitutional amendments.
3. **Calibrate Photo ID Credentials:** If voting in a strict photo ID state (e.g., GA, IN, MS, NC, OH, TN, TX), confirm that your state driver license or passport is valid and unexpired; if unpossessed, obtain a free state voter ID card from your county election office before election week.
4. **Track Mail Ballots Actively:** If voting by mail, use your state's online tracking system (e.g., BallotTrax) to confirm ballot acceptance; if notified of a signature discrepancy, respond immediately to county cure notices.

### For Civic-Tech Developers and Product Architects
1. **Integrate Authoritative Upstream APIs:** Build tools directly upon verified data pipelines: Open FEC API for campaign finance, U.S. Census Geocoder for boundary lookup, and the Voting Information Project (VIP) / Democracy Works for polling place feeds.
2. **Architect for Localized Delegation:** Design applications that gracefully handle county-level bifurcation in states (e.g., Texas, Pennsylvania, Florida, Illinois) where sample ballots and local race data are maintained on county clerk subdomains rather than centralized state APIs.
3. **Enforce Zero-PII Retention:** Protect voter trust by implementing client-side address geocoding and ensuring no voter registration credentials, birthdates, or social security numbers are stored on third-party servers.

### For Journalists and Policy Researchers
1. **Rely on Primary Canvasses:** Avoid citing unofficial election night television projections for margin-of-victory calculations; rely exclusively on certified statement-of-vote canvasses published by state Secretaries of State.
2. **Cross-Check Equipment via Verified Voting:** When reporting on election security and machine irregularities, audit county voting technology through Verified Voting's *The Verifier* database.
3. **Utilize MIT Election Lab Datasets:** Ground historical voter turnout and election administration studies in the peer-reviewed Elections Performance Index (EPI) and MEDSL precinct-level archives.

### For Election Administrators and Policymakers
1. **Expand Online Voter Registration:** The six states lacking open web OVR should enact statutory authority to integrate motor vehicle and state agency databases into secure digital OVR portals.
2. **Deploy Universal Ballot Tracking:** States permitting absentee voting should implement automated ballot tracking (e.g., BallotTrax / Ballot Scout) to reduce administrative inquiry calls and bolster voter confidence.
3. **Standardize Centralized Sample Ballots:** State election divisions should mandate that all county election offices upload machine-readable precinct sample ballots to a centralized state clearinghouse.

---

## 16. Future outlook and scenarios

### 16.1 Trajectory from 2024 to 2026/2028 Election Cycles
- **Expansion of In-Person Early Voting:** Following statutory enactments in Connecticut, Michigan, and New York, in-person early voting on electronic poll books and optical ballot scanners is becoming universal nationwide, blurring the traditional divide between absentee and Election Day voting.
- **Mandatory Paper Audit Trails (VVPAT):** The phase-out of paperless direct recording electronic (DRE) voting machines is nearly complete across all 50 states, ensuring that over 98% of votes cast in the 2026 and 2028 cycles will produce a voter-verified paper audit trail eligible for post-election risk-limiting audits (RLAs).
- **Automated Voter Registration (AVR) Expansion:** Over 24 states have transitioned from passive 'opt-in' Motor Voter registration to automated 'opt-out' AVR at motor vehicle and health care agencies, substantially increasing registration roll completeness.

### 16.2 Technological and Administrative Frontier
- **Artificial Intelligence in Voter Information:** Generative AI search engines and conversational voice assistants are increasingly serving as the primary discovery layer for voters. Ensuring that AI platforms index authoritative `.gov` election sources while filtering out deepfakes and hallucinated polling locations is the central election information challenge of the 2026-2030 era.
- **Cybersecurity Hardening under CISA:** Election infrastructure resilience is shifting toward zero-trust architecture, multi-factor authentication for election worker portals, encrypted endpoint monitoring via Albert sensors, and institutional defense against coordinated distributed denial-of-service (DDoS) attempts.
- **Post-2030 Census Redistricting Horizon:** Demographic shifts toward the Sunbelt (Texas, Florida, North Carolina, Georgia, Arizona) and away from the Northeast and upper Midwest will drive further congressional reapportionment following the 2030 Census, heightening legal scrutiny over independent citizen redistricting commissions versus legislative line-drawing.

---

## 17. Conclusion

The American election ecosystem is a remarkable demonstration of decentralized federalism. While federal constitutional amendments and civil rights statutes establish inviolable legal floors, the operational reality of registering to vote, researching candidates, casting a ballot, and verifying election outcomes is administered across 51 state-level systems and over 10,000 county and municipal jurisdictions.

As demonstrated throughout this directory, digital voter infrastructure has reached unprecedented maturity. 44 states operate automated or online voter registration; over 34 states provide real-time digital ballot tracking; and national nonpartisan platforms like Vote411, Ballotpedia, Vote.org, and Can I Vote provide citizens with unprecedented access to personalized, address-specific electoral intelligence. Nevertheless, stark statutory divides—particularly regarding strict photo identification rules, paper-only registration barriers in seven states, and uneven ballot tracking—mean that a citizen's voting experience remains deeply dependent upon geographic jurisdiction.

By consolidating verified primary `.gov` resources, canonical statutory citations, metropolitan county directories, and nonpartisan research clearinghouses into a single rigorously sourced reference, this directory equips voters, technologists, scholars, and administrators with the essential tools required to navigate, audit, and strengthen American democratic participation.

### Appendix F — Contradiction log
This log documents instances where external aggregators or legacy sources publish conflicting or outdated election information, alongside the verified factual resolution:

| Topic / Entity | Conflicting / Outdated Assertion | Verified Legal Reality | Preferred Authoritative Source |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Idaho Voter ID | Third-party sites claim Idaho allows student IDs and affidavits at polls. | Idaho HB 340 (2023) strictly eliminated student photo IDs and personal affidavits as acceptable credentials. | Idaho Code § 34-1113 / Idaho Secretary of State |
| Missouri Voter ID | Outdated blogs claim Missouri accepts utility bills and bank statements. | Missouri HB 1878 (2022) enacted strict government-issued photo ID; non-photo documents no longer accepted. | RSMo § 115.427 / Missouri Secretary of State |
| North Carolina Voter ID | Several older voter guides claim NC photo ID requirement is permanently enjoined. | The NC Supreme Court overturned injunctions in 2023; photo ID was strictly enforced starting in 2023 elections. | N.C.G.S. § 163-166.16 / NCSBE |
| New Hampshire Voter ID | Legacy directories list NH affidavit voting as an alternative to photo ID. | New Hampshire HB 1569 (2024) repealed the challenged voter affidavit exception for general voting. | RSA § 659:13 / NH Secretary of State |
| Texas Voter Registration | Aggregator listicles frequently claim Texas has open online voter registration. | Texas statute does not permit general open web OVR; online registration is strictly limited to DPS driver license renewals. | Tex. Elec. Code § 13.002 / Texas Secretary of State |
| North Dakota Registration | Unvetted search results claim ND has a 30-day voter registration deadline. | North Dakota is the only state in the nation that has completely abolished voter registration. | N.D.C.C. Title 16.1 / ND Secretary of State |

### Appendix G — Consolidated state portal feature matrix (all 51 jurisdictions)
The table below provides a unified feature comparison across all 50 states and the District of Columbia, auditing seven key voter services:
- **OVR:** Online Voter Registration available.
- **Status:** Voter registration status lookup tool.
- **Polls:** Online polling place / vote center finder.
- **Sample:** Address-specific sample ballot tool.
- **Results:** Official certified election results database.
- **VBM:** Vote-by-mail / absentee application portal.
- **Track:** Automated digital ballot tracking system.
